The Extraordinary Music Worthy of an Extraordinary Life in ‘W.E.B. Du Bois: Rebel with a Cause’

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Rita Coburn‘s “W.E.B. Du Bois: Rebel with a Cause” is an extraordinary documentary about an extraordinary man, a film that spans nearly 100 years in two hours, yet never feels rushed or superficial. In her storytelling, Coburn shares with her subject a talent for distilling complex ideas and expressing them with force and clarity; the result is a movie that fully honors the breadth and depth of Du Bois’ achievements as a groundbreaking Black writer, historian, and activist.

One of Coburn’s most potent tools in this regard is the music by composer Kathryn Bostic, who previously collaborated with the director on “Marian Anderson: The Whole World in Her Hands.” Bostic incorporates a wide array of styles and influences in her score, which references the various types of music Du Bois encountered and appreciated as a Black intellectual but isn’t beholden to them; as evocative of Bach and American folk music as it is of 19th-century spirituals and Asian motifs, it’s the sonic equivalent of Du Bois’ literary works. Related Stories ‘Coward’ Trailer: Lukas Dhont’s Belgian Oscar Entry Is a Queer Stunner 26 Years After He Wrote ‘Meet the Parents,’ John Hamburg Brings Comedy Back to Theaters with ‘Focker In-Law’

The scope of the project required Bostic to come at the material from multiple angles and perspectives. “Rita and I talked about what types of touchstones we needed to define the narrative musically,” Bostic told IndieWire. “There was the regional touchstone, the era-specific touchstone, the interior emotional aspects that we wanted to heighten with music. Then there’s always the discovery process as you begin to create.”

For Bostic, that discovery process is one of the most exciting aspects of film composing. While she had a number of reference points as she began work on the score, she soon found herself following intuition to take them in different directions than initially intended. “There’s always what I call the hands-on-the-clay moment,” Bostic said. “You have your list, and you have your frame of reference, but the process is fluid.”

According to Coburn, Bostic’s ability to bring a historical perspective to her scores made her an ideal collaborator for the film. “Kathryn pulled from the region of Great Barrington [where Du Bois was born] as well as New York in that time period, and then what was happening with the African-American community that Du Bois was entering,” Coburn said. “There wasn’t gospel then, so you were looking at a small group of Black people going to the churches there in New England. What were they listening to, and therefore what was he listening to?”

Bostic drew on the music of the Fisk Jubilee Singers, a Black a capella ensemble that inspired Du Bois, but then moved in a completely different direction to explore his time in Berlin, when he encountered and fell in love with classical music. She also incorporated Wolof music from Senegal and the Gambia in collaboration with Coburn’s daughter, who studied classical music and worked in the music department on “Rebel with a Cause.”

“She found a Senegalese professor online who worked with her so she could sing the Wolof,” Coburn said. “Kathryn and I agreed that we had to put the Wolof in the film because it connects Du Bois to his Ghanaian roots. But we had to find it, and we wanted it to be as authentic as possible.”

For Bostic, the challenge was taking an audience from 2026 and presenting the music in a way that would recreate the way in which Du Bois experienced it over a hundred years earlier. “There’s the historic arc of the times, as well as what he’s experiencing emotionally,” Bostic said. In the places where her score was informed by spirituals and the work of the Fisk Jubilee Singers, the composer tried to tie the emotional subtext to the context in which she knew Du Bois was consuming the music.

“When he went to Fisk University, his mother had recently died,” Bostic said. “So that music was a wellspring for his grieving, and I think that depth of sorrow and the catharsis that resulted from that sorrow inspired the sort of deep self-reflection that became such a deep part of who he was. That self-reflection led to a basic decency and humanity, and even now I think of him as being light years ahead of people in the way he embodied justice. I want to shine a light on that.”

Coburn felt a similar sense of purpose, especially since the more she dug into Du Bois’ life the more she realized how relevant his insights were to America in 2026. “He speaks to this current time,” Coburn said, adding that the importance of Du Bois’ work gave her own work a sense of urgency and responsibility. “I knew that it might be 20 or 30 years before we get another Du Bois documentary. I hope not, but I looked at his life and knew that I had to do the best job that I could. We did everything we could for Du Bois because we felt he had done everything he could for us.”

The greatness of “W.E.B. Du Bois: Rebel with a Cause” is that it brings the viewer into Du Bois’ perspective as his world view continues to expand over a 95-year life, and the music is a major part of that subjectivity.

“Kathryn understands that music is a character in any documentary, and her music embodies the character of W.E.B. Du Bois,” Coburn said. “ In the music, we had to grow with him. We had to expand with him. We had to see that his love of Rembrandt and his understanding of classical music, of music that came from an Asian culture, that all of that was important to him. We were catching up with him, and he was driving us.”

“W.E.B. DuBois: Rebel with a Cause” is currently streaming on the PBS app and website.

U.S. Forest Service releases Bigfoot files

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

🛍️ 200+ Black Friday deals actually worth buying 🛍️

Published Nov 24, 2026 1:28 PM EST

The United States Forest Service has quietly published a handful of documents related to multiple, decades’ old Bigfoot sightings. While the files uploaded to the agency’s Freedom of Information Act online reading room may not offer definitive proof of the cryptid’s existence, they may help redirect unwanted political conversations at the Thanksgiving table this year.

“I don’t know if I believe in the Bigfoot theory, but the tracks were right there,” an 87-year-old resident of Sanders County, Montana, told his local newspaper in 2003 after discovering some suspicious footprints. “What else could it be?”

Another file also focuses on purported cryptid tracks in the summer of 1982. In that case, park rangers were actually dispatched to obtain plaster molds of footprints reported in Oregon’s Umatilla National Forest.Unfortunately, resource staffer Roger Baker didn’t sound impressed by what he found on June 17, 1982.

“It is my opinion that the tracks…were made by something other than a 300-pound-plus, two footed being,” Baker explained, although he did concede that the prints appeared “very natural.”

The final archival document is a local resident’s somewhat rambling personal account of the history of Petty Creek, a forested area located about 30 miles west of Missoula, Montana. Among stories of ill-fated gold prospectors, camel importation, and buried treasure, Leo Kinney tells of his own near-brush with Bigfoot. According to Kinney, he and some fellow hunters didn’t actually see the hairy biped walking around, but they saw evidence of its recent presence.

“Tracks, a strong odor, and grunting were observed,” he wrote in 1980.

The U.S. Forestry Service’s archival uploads are fun reads, but many Sasquatch believers are still reeling from the recent release of Capturing Bigfoot. Released in October, the critically acclaimed documentary delves into the story behind the Patterson–Gimlin tape. The controversial home video recording is widely considered the most famous Bigfoot sighting of all time, although the film essentially confirms once-and-for-all that the grainy footage was a carefully constructed hoax.

And, during his appearance on “Late Night with Seth Meyers” Monday to promote the James Gray film “Paper Tiger,” the Oscar-nominated actor was asked a series of questions by the talk-show host about the upcoming superhero movie. First, Meyers held up a photo of Mister Sinister — body suit, sharp collar, shredded cape, makeup and all — to which Driver quipped, “[Looks] just like me. This is 6 a.m. when my daughter wakes me up.”

HBO's 8-Part Thriller Masterpiece Is Aging Like Fine Wine

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Certain franchise TV shows suffer from longevity for a variety of reasons, but an eight-episode thriller on HBO is the complete opposite. The ever-increasing investment in shared universes has sometimes led to disappointing end products, especially those that rely far too much on tying to wider narratives or character arcs. For instance, several MCU TV shows have had this issue, be it Echo's reliance on characters from Netflix's Marvel shows or She-Hulk's attempts to explore the franchise in a meta way.

Still, there are others that age well. HBO, in particular, is a studio that has several examples of this; The recent Lanterns, despite the DC Universe's future now being in doubt, is one of HBO's best sci-fi shows and is a self-contained story that doesn't require wider viewing. This is just one example of HBO's ability to produce TV shows that work in their own right, despite being part of a wider universe. However, even Lanterns could age poorly, given James Gunn's recent exit from DC Studios.

Conversely, one HBO show that is aging like fine wine is an eight-part story that still exists within a wider continuity, but operates as a concise, well-written, utterly compelling thriller with its own narrative themes and weight. That show is The Penguin, 2024's spin-off of Matt Reeves' The Batman. The Penguin exists within Reeves' Batman Epic Crime Saga, but it works on its own, meaning it doesn't rely on The Batman, its upcoming sequel, or any other entries in the franchise.

This alone renders The Penguin a show that will age well, as it could be watched 10 years from now with no prior viewing and still work as an excellent, character-driven thriller. Beyond that, though, The Penguin will avoid the aforementioned issue with some shared universe entries, as it doesn't need the wider franchise to succeed. With all of the uncertainty surrounding DC at the moment, shows tied to the wider narrative like Lanterns or, in an even more extreme case, Peacemaker, may see lingering threads fizzle out. As its own entity, however, The Penguin will age well with or without the wider DC franchise.

The future of DC being so unsettled raises questions about The Batman - Part III, while Part II remains plagued with production issues. The latter recently paused production due to Matt Reeves' personal issues, with Steven Soderbergh taking over to finish filming. This indicates The Batman - Part II will still be released in February 2028, but the impending shake-up at DC could mean the planned third installment will not happen, especially with Reeves facing so many issues making the second movie.

When looking at The Penguin, it will not matter whether The Batman franchise continues after Part II. The tight, eight-episode narrative wraps everything up perfectly, with only the subtle hints and Easter eggs to the events of The Batman being woven into it effortlessly, without any need to know the context. Moreover, The Batman - Part II is not expected to feature the character of Oz Cobb all that extensively, meaning The Penguin will only continue to age well, no matter the fate of its wider franchise.

All of this just proves that The Penguin nailed how to be part of a shared universe, only as its own spin-off. As alluded to, some franchises fail to find this balance, with even some of Marvel's better-rated shows, like Ms. Marvel, being essential viewing before The Marvels or, presumably, the upcoming Avengers: Secret Wars. The Penguin, on the other hand, feels like its own thing. Yes, it has connections to The Batman, but the central character arcs and wider narrative are not directly impacted by the movie, nor does it set up any sequels with lingering plot threads.

The Penguin can still tie into future stories, should they so wish to be constructed as a sequel of sorts. Still, it remains the case that this will be a decision of different filmmakers, as opposed to The Penguin's creative team actively writing the show to tie it to future stories. Instead, The Penguin was constructed to be its own entity, with its own unique story, characters, and eight-episode narrative.

‘Elephants in the Fog’ Adds Mexico, South Korea, Portugal Sales as Oscar Campaign Visits U.N. (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Best Friend Forever has closed new sales deals for “Elephants in the Fog” in Mexico, South Korea and Portugal, as Nepal’s Oscar entry for best international feature film builds a campaign around impact screenings that place the Kinnar community it portrays at the center of policy conversations.

Tulip will distribute in Mexico, Film Dabin in South Korea and Leopardo Filmes in Portugal. Talks for North American rights are under way. The new deals bring the film’s total to 17 territories, following earlier agreements covering the U.K., Spain, Benelux, Switzerland, Israel, Australia, India, Indonesia, the Philippines, France, Germany and Austria, Brazil, Nepal and inflight rights with Silk Route. Related Stories Here's How to Watch WWE Crown Jewel 2026 Live Online UFC's Event Sellout Spree Powers TKO Q3 Earnings

The campaign’s most recent stop was U.N. Headquarters in New York, where the film screened on Nov. 20 – Transgender Day of Remembrance. The event was co-hosted by the Permanent Missions of Nepal, Norway, France and Brazil to the United Nations, together with Outright International. Gyan Bahadur Magar, Nepal’s Deputy Permanent Representative to the United Nations, opened the evening by honoring those killed for their identity and paying tribute to community organizations including Blue Diamond Society, Nepal’s leading LGBTIQ+ rights organization, for their response to the country’s August floods despite severe resource constraints. “LGBTIQ+ persons must be partners in shaping decisions, not merely beneficiaries of policies,” he said. “Remembrance without action is not enough. Recognition without protection is not enough.”

Lead actress Pushpa Thing Lama – an activist with Blue Diamond Society since 2005 – addressed the room in a video message. The panel that followed, “Leaving No One Behind: Including LGBTI People in Humanitarian Response,” featured Anjali Lama, Nepal’s first transgender model; Vivek Rai, deputy director of the Civil Society Division at UN Women; André du Plessis, U.N. program director of Outright International; and Ana Seco Díaz, the film’s impact producer. Du Plessis said trans people are too often discussed at the United Nations and in politics as an ideology, making it all the more important to put real people in front of those debates. “Inclusion is not charity. It is a matter of human rights,” said Anjali Lama.

The screening connected the film to a broader humanitarian argument. When floods and landslides struck Nepal in August, 987 people were killed and 3,916 left missing, and the country’s gender and sexual minority communities found that legal protections on the books did not reliably carry through into emergency response. A report presented to the General Assembly last month by Graeme Reid, the U.N. Independent Expert on sexual orientation and gender identity, found that LGBT people face compounding exclusion during humanitarian crises. Reid said that trans people are too often invisible in emergency systems, and that the people most affected by a crisis must be part of shaping the response to it. Following cuts to USAID, Blue Diamond Society laid off 262 of its 350 staff and was forced to close 21 of its clinics.

“If the film clears even a little of the fog of prejudice, allowing someone to truly see another person, or perhaps even a part of themselves, then I feel the work has done something meaningful,” director Abinash Bikram Shah said.

The campaign continues on Dec. 1 – World AIDS Day – in London at the Arzner, the U.K.’s LGBTQ+ cinema, in partnership with the Terrence Higgins Trust and Amnesty U.K.’s Queer Rights Network, with funds raised going to the Trust and to Blue Diamond Society. Members of Parliament have been invited to hear how cuts to international aid have hit community-led organizations like Blue Diamond Society. On Dec. 3, Film Independent takes the film to Los Angeles for its Programmers’ Picks series.

The Cannes Un Certain Regard Jury Prize winner is Nepal’s submission in this year’s Oscar international feature race. Since its Cannes premiere it has also won the Grand Jury Prize at NewFest. Pushpa Thing Lama received the best performance prize at the Asia Pacific Screen Awards. In France, the film has surpassed 80,000 admissions, an unprecedented figure for a Nepali release.

“Elephants in the Fog” is directed by Abinash Bikram Shah, whose short “Lori” received a Special Mention at the Cannes Short Film Competition in 2022. It is a Nepal, France, Germany, Brazil and Norway co-production by Underground Talkies Nepal, Les Valseurs and Die Gesellschaft DGS, with co-producers Enquadramento Produções, Bubbles Project, Zischlermann Filmproduktion, Storm Films and Jayantii Creations. International sales are handled by Best Friend Forever.

The film is playing at the International Film Festival of India in Goa.

Momentous 61st Venice Biennale Ends With 2nd-Highest Visitor Turnout

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Political disputes, a historic strike, and the curator's sudden passing did not keep visitors away.

The 61st Venice Biennale, curated by the late Koyo Kouoh, concluded yesterday, November 22, reporting 714,015 visitors during its six-month run. Nearly 30,000 visitors attended the pre-opening of In Minor Keys, which included a record-high 100 participating countries.

Despite running three weeks shorter than previous editions to allow for the installation of the renovated Central Pavilion at the Giardini, In Minor Keys is the second highest-attended exhibition in the event's history. (The 2022 edition holds the record, counting more than 800,000 visitors.) The news follows the highest-ever visitor numbers at the most recent editions of the Venice Architecture Biennale and Venice International Film Festival, two other landmark events organized under the La Biennale di Venezia.

Following Kouoh's unprecedented death in May 2025 at the age of 57, her team of collaborators — Rasha Salti, Marie Hélène Pereira, Gabe Beckhurst Feijoo, Siddhartha Mitter, and Rory Tsapayi — carried out the exhibition of 109 international artists, many of whom hailed from Africa and the Caribbean diasporas.

Louisa Jacobson’s Stylist on 10 Things That Inspire Her Red Carpet Looks

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

She’s been on a winning style streak—here’s a glimpse at the conversations behind the ensembles

In fashion, anything from niche archival research to a favorite film to an outfit you saw on your morning commute can make for inspiration. In our new series 10 Things, we ask industry professionals and tastemakers to divulge the (you guessed it) 10 things that had the most impact on their aesthetic choices.

Method dressing has become the road most traveled for Hollywood A-listers and their stylists. We’ve seen Zendaya and Law Roach turn out goddess-like dresses in homage to the actress’s role of Athena in The Odyssey, while the cast of the Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping has been channeling their outlandish onscreen personas into equally over-the-top red carpet looks. But even though period pieces present a prime opportunity for imitation, The Gilded Age star Louisa Jacobson, who plays Marian Brook in the Julian Fellowes series, says she’d rather dress like herself.

“One thing that we are always aware of is not being the character she plays on TV, because she is more than that,” Jacobson’s stylist Edward Bowleg III told Harper’s Bazaar over Zoom. “She is a fully fleshed-out human being. She is Louisa and she is not Marian. That is an important distinction.”

Bowleg started working with the actress in 2022 after a mutual friend introduced them right around the time that The Gilded Age was set to premiere. The editorial stylist, who has worked for countless publications and brands like Hermès, Valentino, and Marc Jacobs, recalls his first official project with Jacobson: the 2022 Vanity Fair Oscars party, to which she wore an ivory off-the-shoulder Khaite gown with a Jeffrey Levinson clutch.

Much has changed in the four years since that evening. “When she cut her hair short, that was the moment where a lot shifted,” said Bowleg. “It was a new her.” With the fresh haircut came a slew of delightfully unexpected red carpet moments, spanning a handful of tailored suits (and a cummerbund!) from Haider Ackermann’s Tom Ford, Conner Ives, and Calvin Klein Collection to a Dilara Findikoglu minidress with an armored bodice made from safety pins, metal accents, and a tattered chiffon train.

Jacobson’s fashion evolution paralleled her own self-actualization. “Exploring sexual identity and being more free with that led to having more fun with experimental fashion and being unapologetic about what she likes and who she is,” said Bowleg. And it shows: She’s been on a winning style streak recently.

Coming off Jacobson’s recent Gilded Age press tour, which saw looks from Haider Ackermann’s Tom Ford, Ashlyn, Commission, and Mel Usine, Bowleg sat down with Bazaar to decode 10 influences that shaped his fashion choices, from Saint Laurent’s le smoking to an Azzedine Alaïa collection that lives rent-free in his head.

“We love tailoring, we love a suit. I could have her in a suit every day of the week. She went to her first Tom Ford show in March. When she told me she was invited, I was like ‘Oh my god, that is so cool,’ because I am a big fan. The 1996 Gucci show is burned into my brain. That is when my interest in fashion really began, so for me in a weird way it was almost like a full circle moment.

“I think with creative director Haider Ackermann’s tailoring in particular, the cut is so exquisite. When we have fittings with them, the only thing we are adjusting is the length of the trousers. There is also a confidence that she has when she puts them on. It’s very commanding. Then you mix that in with all of the elements—there is always a really sexy shoe that goes along with it.”

“An image that could have been on the moodboard would be Helmut Newton’s photos of Saint Laurent’s Le Smoking with the women on the street in Paris. There is one image with a naked woman wearing just heels, standing with another woman in Le Smoking. That is very much the kind of vibe we are going for: mixing something sexy and feminine with tailoring, which is inherently masculine.”

“She went to the Met Gala this year with Dilara [Findikoglu]. When I had the conversation with Dilara and her team, the idea of armor came up. She had been to the Met ahead of our conversation, and she sent me all of these pictures that she had taken in the Medieval section where they have the helmets and the chain mail and the body armor. Those were just things that she loved and that became a big inspiration for the look.”

“For Louisa’s first Met Gala in 2022 (the red carpet theme was ‘Gilded Glamour, White Tie’) there was a John Singer Sargent painting of a woman in a black dress. The image helped direct our choice of look”—a futuristic gown with a gold trompe l'œil skeleton molded in leather and hand-painted in gold leaf from the Schiaparelli Spring/Summer 2022 Haute Couture collection by Daniel Roseberry.

“I love Azzedine Alaïa. Specifically the 1986 show—the one with all of the bandage dressing with the cut-outs on the sides and the infamous denim and leather zip that goes all across the body. I believe Grace Jones walked. Even with the McQueen that she just wore, in a way it’s that sort of vibe—the bomber jacket but with the little skirt. It’s that kind of energy: A strong, powerful woman.”

“There has been quite a bit of leather. The McQueen that she just wore, the Khaite full leather suit that she wore to the Vanity Fair Oscars party. She put it on, and it was like ‘This is fab.’ We were wondering if it was right for Vanity Fair and whether she would be the only one in a suit. I told Louisa that Zendaya had worn a suit the year before and I also was kind of like, ‘Fuck it, you look hot, this is great.’ Then there were two more leather Louis Vuitton looks that she wore. One was to the Vanity Fair Oscars party in 2024. And then before that, she wore a Vuitton look to the SAG Awards and the whole bodice was sculpted in leather, as well.”

“Hedi Slimane is a big inspiration of mine. Again, it’s a lot of tailoring, it’s leather, it’s a great silhouette. It’s the aesthetic overall, but in particular, it was the fall/winter 2006 collection that he did. There were things that were skinny and then there were things that were a bit more loose in terms of the trousers. There was a softness to the hardness of it all. It was still very edgy and a bit rock ‘n’ roll, but there was a soft glamour to it. That is something that I like with Louisa, even when it comes to the jewelry.”

“I am really curious to see what Pieter Mulier will do with Versace and Atelier Versace is in January. [I should also say that] I am obsessed with Donatella Versace. When I started looking at all things [high] fashion, Donatella was another designer that I loved. That glamour and excess and sexiness—she is unapologetically who she is.”

“With music and ‘90s hip-hop, you look at the bling, the diamonds, and things like that. I love a great jewelry moment, so accessorizing Louisa’s looks with the right jewelry is always important part to me. I love diamonds. When she recently went to a Tiffany & Co. event [in Ashlyn], we initially had our brooch up on the lapel where you would traditionally see it. Then she was like, ‘It doesn’t feel right.’ So I moved it down to the peplum of the jacket. It was a big, big yellow diamond, which I was obsessed with. I just loved how much it sparkled and shined.”

“Helmut Lang is also a big inspiration. There is a Spring 1998 men's collection and a Spring 1999 collection that I think about a lot in terms of silhouette and things that still resonate today. It all comes from having researched some of those collections in the past, so they tend to be recurring elements in the work.”

10 Saddest Deaths in 'Sunrise on the Reaping,' Ranked

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Spoiler Alert: This list contains spoilers for The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping.The Hunger Games has never been shy about putting its characters through absolute misery, and Sunrise on the Reaping is no exception. Set during the 50th year of the Games—its Second Quarter Quell—the latest chapter in Suzanne Collins' dystopian saga follows a young Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada) as he's thrown into an arena designed to be twice as deadly as the usual Games, with 48 tributes fighting for survival.

But as brutal as the competition itself is, it's the people Haymitch loses along the way that make this story truly one of the most devastating additions to the franchise. From friendships forged in the arena to the loved ones left behind in District 12, Sunrise on the Reaping is filled with so many deaths that have left a mark on the young tribute as well as audiences alike. So, here are some of the most poignant ones.

Panache Barker (Jhaleil Swaby) is one of the District 1 Career tributes who enters the Quarter Quell with a clear advantage over many of the other participants. Having already trained since young, he quickly established himself as a dangerous threat, with his arrogance, brute strength, and aggression already creating conflict before the games even began. But Panache's confidence ultimately proves to be his downfall in the arena, as Maysilee Donner (Mckenna Grace) uses her poisoned darts to stealthily kill him, right as he's about to deliver a fatal attack on Haymitch.

Having built himself up as the ruthless antagonist of the games, Panache's sudden takedown isn't particularly heartbreaking on its own. In fact, it initially feels like a moment of relief and dark irony, since he was, after all, constantly brutalizing the other tributes—and seemingly enjoying it. However, despite his actions, there remains an underlying tragedy as Panache is simply just another teenager who was manipulated by the Capitol into becoming a cruel killer. A mere cog in the endless machine.

As another District 1 Career, Silka Sharp (Laura Marcus) was undoubtedly ruthless and skilled—so much so that she became one of the last tributes standing in the arena alongside Haymitch. Their final confrontation is a tense battle that leaves Haymitch badly wounded, and Silka seemingly having the upper hand. But when she corners him towards the area's edge, Haymitch uses the hidden force field to his advantage, taunting her to throw her axe and have the weapon rebound to fatally strike her.

Similar to Panache, Silka's death isn't necessarily upsetting, as it's staged as a pure, triumphant "outsmarting the villain" climax. That's what happens when she's consistently positioned as a lethal powerhouse who's actively gunning to spill blood and win. But she, too, is a tragic product of the Capitol's cruel system. Unfortunately, the movie omits an important book moment of vulnerability that showcased this (yes, we're talking about the chocolate scene).

Wellie (Rada Rae) is one of the Newcomers from District 6, and as one of the youngest competitors in the arena, it's understandable why Haymitch was quick to care for her before the games began. We see this as he offers Wellie his lunch during one of the tribute's meal sessions. Unfortunately, we don't get much from her after that, as her death occurs off-screen with Haymitch finding her body after Silka rings Wellie's bicycle bell as a means to lure him into an ambush.

While the discovery is obviously grim and heartbreaking, audiences who haven't read the book have very little time to get attached to Wellie before she dies. In fact, the film effectively glosses over the important bond forged between Haymitch and Wellie in the game. They even soften her actual death—which was arguably the most gruesome and brutal one of all. Nevertheless, her death still remains an emotional gut punch as she symbolizes the tragic loss of innocents.

Entering the Second Quarter Quell alongside his other District 12 tributes, Wyatt Callow (Ben Wang) quickly becomes a sympathetic figure. Despite the initial hostility surrounding his family's role as oddsmakers—people who took bets on the Hunger Games—Wyatt earns the trust of his fellow tributes and proves that he's willing to protect those who are more vulnerable than himself. Of course, we see this right at the start of the games, during the chaotic Cornucopia bloodbath, as he steps in front of Silka's axe-throw to shield young Lou Lou (Iona Bell).

In this case, his death is truly upsetting because it comes from an act of kindness and sacrifice rather than selfishness or ambition. Wyatt isn't trying to win at someone else's expense. He simply wants to protect Lou Lou, a girl who has already been subjected to the Capitol's cruel manipulation. His willingness to put himself in harm's way makes him an easy character to root for, and his death only reinforces how little room the Games leave for such compassion.

Lou Lou is a tragic figure from the moment she is introduced, as she's essentially sent in as a mere body-double since the Capitol had to cover up the accidental death of Louella McCoy (Molly McCann). Seemingly drugged (or at least conditioned) into playing a role she doesn't fully understand, the girl is thrust into the Games without any control over her own circumstances. Despite surviving the initial bloodbath, she meets her end when she inhales poisonous pollen from the deadly plants scattered throughout the arena.

Naturally, what makes Lou Lou's death so tragic is the sheer indignity of her existence in the Games. For one, her fate was immediately set as she was sent in as a sacrificial pawn—one who doesn't even get to die under her real name. The books at least give hints about her past, but they also reinforce the disturbing lengths the Capitol is willing to go to in order to maintain the illusion of its spectacle. Lou Lou is one of the many victims of a system that sees children as disposable, and her fate is a reminder that the cruelty of the Games extends far beyond the arena's physical dangers.

The tragedy of Louella McCoy is how her death happens before she even gets the chance to enter the arena. During the tribute parade, the horses pulling the chariots are spooked by objects thrown onto the track, causing them to panic and crash. Haymitch is thrown off, while Louella is sadly left trapped in the District 12 chariot, which flips over and drags her across the path. It's brutal to watch and utterly shocking, especially since it sets the stage for Haymitch to boil with anger and openly defy President Snow (Ralph Fiennes).

Incredibly young and never even given the opportunity to fight for her own life, Louella is killed by the very spectacle that the Capitol insists is meant to honor them. If anything, it exposes just how careless and cruel the system (and its people) truly are, particularly since they flippantly replace her with another girl the next day. By erasing her loss, the Capitol once again strips a child of their dignity, just for the sake of maintaining the illusion of control.

Despite her sharp-tongued nature, Maysilee Donner grows to become one of Haymitch's closest allies as they fight to protect each other across the area. But as the number of tributes begins to dwindle, the two consciously decide to go their separate ways as neither wants to watch the other die (or be responsible for it). Moments later, a cannon fires, and they rush back towards each other, only for a flock of bird mutts to fatally attack Maysilee, with Haymitch scrambling to protect her in his arms.

Of course, the moment is agonizing to watch as Maysilee isn't just another tribute Haymitch loses along the way. She's a slice of home and a true friend who shares his determination to survive without surrendering completely to the brutality of the Capitol. Yes, there are significant changes from the book that shape her death as collateral punishment for Haymitch rather than a direct consequence of her own acts of rebellion. However, the stellar performance between Zada and Grace makes up for this omission, with their chemistry carrying the emotional weight of a relationship built on genuine care and humanity—even as they're in a competition designed to strip its participants of precisely that.

As the son of the legendary victor, Beetee (Kelvin Harrison Jr.), Ampert Latier (Percy Daggs IV) is a clever and resourceful young boy who helps recruit Haymitch to sabotage the Games as a means to undermine the Capitol's control. Their plan succeeds in disrupting the system, but the Capitol retaliates by unleashing vicious squirrel mutts on Ampert. Haymitch finds him being attacked and desperately tries to intervene. Sadly, the creatures horrifically overwhelm the young tribute, leaving Haymitch unable to save him.

Ampert's death is particularly tragic because he symbolizes the devastating risks of daring to challenge an authoritarian regime. Like some others, the young boy's fate of death was already sealed thanks to his father's prior acts of rebellion. As a result, his courage and willingness to risk everything for a chance at freedom only makes his loss all the more shattering—especially since it happens in such a brutal manner (one that's even worse and more gruesome in the books).

After unexpectedly surviving the Second Quarter Quell and returning to District 12 as a victor, Haymitch is confronted with the consequences of his defiance: his home is engulfed in flames where both his mother (Kara Tointon) and little brother, Sid (Smylie Bradwell), were trapped inside. Of course, it's clear that President Snow has made good on his threats, with Haymitch having nothing to do but watch as his entire family is taken from him. To make matters worse, the tragedy becomes even more painful when they reveal that mother and son were found holding onto each other, resulting in them being buried in the same casket.

There's something especially cruel about this punishment, as Haymitch's family are true innocents. Neither knew of his plans, and yet they were forced to pay the price. In this sense, Snow isn't merely trying to silence a rebellious victor. He's making an example of Haymitch and ensuring that his victory comes with a lifetime of suffering. The loss of Ma and Sid also strips Haymitch of the very support system he desperately needs after the Games, leaving him isolated and increasingly broken. It's a deeply personal act of vengeance, and one that helps explain why Haymitch eventually retreats into deep despair.

Just when you think Haymitch has lost so much, he's met with another devastating blow. After the funeral of his family and fellow tributes, he finally reunites with his long-love, Lenore Dove Baird (Whitney Peak), who has finally been released from captivity. And for a fleeting moment, their reunion offers a glimmer of hope as she happily pulls out a bag of gumdrops she found on the rocks, thinking them to be a gift. Haymitch, in turn, feeds one to her—only for him to quickly realize that it was a trap planted by Snow. Once again, he's left helpless in his desperation and can do nothing but watch someone he loves die in his arms.

Her death is distressing not just because it's Lenore Dove, but because of what she represents to Haymitch. Throughout the film, she's one of the few people who genuinely understands his anger at the Capitol and even encourages him to believe things can change. She isn't simply a romantic interest, but a person whose conviction and rebellious spirit help shape the person Haymitch becomes. Even as she dies, Lenore Dove makes him promise to keep fighting against the oppressive system, turning her final moments into one last act of defiance. And knowing what Haymitch's emotional journey looks like in the future makes this loss all the more heartbreaking as audiences realize that it's his memories of the people he loved that have kept him going.

‘The Gilded Age’ Just Used a TV Trope We Really Need to Retire

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Spoilers for The Gilded Age season 4 ahead.

Oh, Marian. Marian, Marian, Marian. We finally got to see her letting loose, and then it all came crashing down. Hard. As you may have seen coming from a mile away, on Sunday night’s fourth episode of The Gilded Age season 4, Marian realized that she was pregnant (at least she assumes she is). After a one-night stand. I think I’ve seen this show before—and it was created and written by the same person.

The season 4 premiere shows Marian Brook going out on the town with her ex-fiancé Larry Russell following Enid and Oscar’s wedding. They have a great time dancing, and then they have a great time at a hotel after bad weather means they can’t make the trip back uptown. Marian literally lets her Victorian-era hair down and loses her virginity to the man she loves—or at least, loved.

The aftermath is not fun, though. Marian thinks of herself as a ruined woman for having premarital sex, and Larry offers to get re-engaged to her—a fine idea considering they both still have feelings for each other—but she sees it as a pity re-proposal and refuses him. A few episodes later, she’s having nosebleeds all over the place and admitting to a colleague at the settlement house that she hasn’t had her “monthly courses.” (We’ll get to more on why I don’t like this storyline, but I will give the show a shoutout for using the underrepresented pregnancy symptom of nosebleeds rather than the classic barfing everywhere.)

This could have been different. Marian, a character who has had happiness snatched out of her hands time and time again, could have just stubbornly continued to turn down Larry and devoted herself to her work, only to realize by the season finale that she actually is still in love with him. I wouldn’t have minded a good old slow burn here! Much of this show moves so fast. Marian and Larry’s romance could’ve kept things more grounded.

But, instead, we get the classic TV trope of “woman gets pregnant after having sex with someone once.” And this particular situation has the added bonus of “and she was a virgin.” Shows as wide-ranging as Friends, Mad Men, The Secret Life of the American Teenager, Reign, Ginny & Georgia, and The Originals have featured one-night stands that lead to pregnancies to varying levels of success. The trope is supposed to make us gasp, but at this point, it’s just kind of cheap and predictable.

Most notably, this already happened on Downton Abbey, which, like The Gilded Age, was written and created by Julian Fellowes. The character Edith—who is very similar to Marian in that she repeatedly gets into doomed relationships with men and devotes herself to work at a time when that’s not the norm for aristocratic women—gets pregnant after sleeping with her love interest, Michael Gregson, one time. As with The Gilded Age, given the time period of the show, this was not happy news and was just another blow to a character who kept getting the short end of the stick. (Speaking of the short end of the stick, Michael Gregson goes out of the country following their lovemaking and dies while away, so Edith never even gets to see him again!)

The whole “Poor Edith” thing became something of a running joke for Downton fans and on the actual show, which was entertaining, but seeing something so similar happen again on The Gilded Age just makes me wish this had gone in a more creative direction. Maybe Marian could’ve kept hooking up with Larry despite the side of herself that knew it was a bad idea. Maybe Marian could’ve sworn off of men altogether. (Honestly, it’d make sense at this point! I mean, damn, that’s three failed engagements!) Anything but pregnancy.

So, where does Poor Marian go from here? My guess, it’ll either be: 1. She isn’t actually pregnant, but the stress from her guilt caused the nosebleeds and missed “monthly courses.” 2. She has the baby and secretly puts it up for adoption a la Edith from Downton. 3. She gets back with Larry, has the baby, and Bertha does some of her conniving to get society to not ask questions about the timeline. 4. She has a miscarriage.

Are any of these options that great? Is this going to be worth it? A miscarriage would mean it was just for the shock (or, “shock”) of the episode 4 cliffhanger. A baby with Larry would be forcing them together more than the guilt of the one-night stand.

Despite my critique of this particular storyline, I love this show, so I am interested to see how it turns out. The creativity might not have been there when it came to using this trope again, but hopefully, it’ll be there in the outcome.

Here’s What A Company Desperate To Win Back Players Looks Like

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It’s been so fascinating to see how various games have been trying to draw attention in the lead-up to and wake ofÂ GTA 6. Fortnite famously has introduced its pirate token, offering those players who find it free stuff from the game’s item shop. Final Fantasy XIV has launched a promotion in which lapsed players can log in for free for 30 days. And Love and Deepspace has deployed the catboys.

To be clear, this is a rerun of an earlier event but it is one of the most popular, beloved events the game has ever run. Love and Deepspace is a gacha game, and the purpose of the event is to get players to spend money on “pulls,” or chances to win a “memory” or little interactive story bit in which one of the five main characters has been magically transformed into a cat. They have ears and tails, they will nuzzle you and purr, and I can bet there are plenty of suggestive lines of dialogue about wearing collars because the game’s writers are very good about innuendo.

Reruns in gacha games happen all the time. In Love and Deepspace, reruns have typically been for single-character events. The timing of this event and the choice to repeat an event with such legendary status within the community suggests to me that Infold Games is hurting-hurting as the Valko boycott continues into its fifth month, with GTA 6 also blowing a hole in player attention.

And if you think about it, while on paper GTA 6 shouldn’t be competing with Love and Deepspace (beyond the fact that it’s competing with every video game right now), Rockstar’s epic crime drama has boyfriend-simulator elements that could strike right at the heart of your average LADS enjoyer. Much of the discourse around GTA 6 right now is about how much of a good boyfriend Jason can be to Lucia. He fixes up their shared house, buys her gifts, and is enthusiastically supportive of Lucia’s criminal aspirations. You can dress him, change his hair, and go on dates with him. He naturally responds to Lucia, calls her pretty, sleeps with her, and professes his love for her. You can have similar interactions with the men of LADS, so it certainly sounds like something a LADS player, especially one still disaffected over the Valko situation, would enjoy.

And catboys are not the only “in case of emergency” feature Love and Deepspace is deploying. Sylus, generally considered the most popular love interest of the bunch, is getting a big update to his story which will include the reveal of his dragon aspect. Sylus’ dragon persona has been revealed to the game’s female main character in the past, but that was in another timeline. (Don’t ask me to make too much sense out of LADS‘ story.) This would be the first acknowledgement of that personality in the current timeline, letting the game put a big, sexy half-dragon man in front of a community of people who read dragon-shifter romantasy books.

'Percy Jackson and the Olympians' Season 4 Fate Revealed Ahead of Season 3 Finale

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Lade Omotade is a News and Features Author at Collider, covering everything from casting announcements and streaming trends to behind-the-scenes stories and the cultural impact of today's biggest film and TV franchises. Since joining the team in 2024, she has written more than 1,000 news stories, with her work also cited by outlets like AOL and IMDb.

Three days ago, Disney+'s beloved fantasy series returned for its third season, earning critics' approval with a perfect 100% score on Rotten Tomatoes. Premiering about ten months after the epic Season 2 finale, Percy Jackson and the Olympians Season 3 is expected to conclude on December 11, following its predecessors with an eight-episode run. The new season has not only won over critics but also brought the show back into the spotlight, as it now dominates streaming charts globally. This, of course, includes the U.S., where it has ranked among the top five over the past 48 hours.

While Season 3 is only two episodes in, a fan-favourite star seems to have hinted at the fate of Percy Jackson and the Olympians Season 4. Keep in mind that an official confirmation is still pending, but there's still plenty of time. In the meantime, as fans know, that wouldn't be unusual: Season 2 of the Disney+ adaptation wasn't announced until February 2024, after Season 1 ended in late January. Season 3, however, was announced earlier, in March 2025, months before Season 2 premiered that December.

During a recent interview at the Empire State Building about Season 3 of Percy Jackson & the Olympians, Walker Scobell discussed the Greek mythology show's future. Scobell, who has played the titular character since the show began, hinted that there will be a Season 4, saying:

"Let's say we don't get another season. I'm just going to binge all of Percy just to watch it. But since, like, I know we're coming back next season, I can't."

Created by Rick Riordan and Jonathan E. Steinberg, Percy Jackson and the Olympians is based on Riordan's bestselling book series, which began with The Lightning Thief in 2005. Three seasons in, Riordan remains optimistic that the adventure series adaptation will continue, and he previously shared this optimism, noting that Season 2 wasn't greenlit early either. According to the author, while some people are panicking over the lack of a Season 4 announcement, the future remains uncertain.

“We've never finished a season with, like, a guaranteed coming back. But at the same time, it's kind of like ... it would be weird to not come back. It would be weird to not complete this.”

Percy Jackson and the Olympians Season 3 adapts the third book, The Titan's Curse, as the titular demigod returns with his friends for a dangerous quest to rescue his demigod friend Annabeth Chase (Leah Sava Jeffries) and the Greek goddess Artemis (Dafne Keen, who joined in Season 3), both kidnapped by Kronos's army. The fantasy drama also stars Aryan Simhadri, Charlie Bushnell, Nick Boraine, and Jason Mantzoukas, along with Levi Chrisopulos and Holt McCallany, who also joined in Season 3.

Percy Jackson and the Olympians streams on Disney+ and Hulu.

One Variant Of Marvel's Doctor Doom Is A Descendant Of A Brutal Historical Villain

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In the 2000s, Marvel Comics launched "Ultimate Marvel," a reboot that brought its lead characters into the 21st century. "Ultimate Fantastic Four" pivoted from from the classic "astronauts bathed in cosmic rays" origin (which is so 1960s). Instead, the "Ultimate" FF were teen geniuses transformed in a teleportation experiment.

"Ultimate Fantastic Four" also reimagined the FF's nemesis, Doctor Doom. Renamed Victor Van Damme, this Doom was part of the Baxter Building think tank and was also transformed in the Four's accident. This Doom has no armor, but literal metal skin. (You can see that both the 2005 and 2015 "Fantastic Four" films took from "Ultimate Fantastic Four" for their Dooms, to their detriment.)

"Ultimate Fantastic Four" #7 (by Warren Ellis and Stuart Immonen) depicts Victor's childhood. On his tenth birthday, Victor's father revealed to him that they are descendants of Vlad Țepeș, or Vlad the Impaler/Vlad Dracula, the infamous 15th century king of Wallachia. Van Damme Sr. explains to his boy:

"Vlad Dracula understood that truth is inborn. That men of greatness must bend an ignorant world to their truth by any means necessary. And so he bent Wallachia to his will. He was cruel, but cruelty is no vice. It is a tool of necessity."

From that moment, Victor was told he must be a man who bends the world to his will. His father instructs him to memorize his family line from Vlad down, beating him whenever he fails. Doom being the descendant of royalty, coming from money, and being imparted from childhood with lessons that a great leader is a ruthless one are all big changes to his character. Victor von Doom acts like an aristocrat, but unlike Victor Van Damme, he came from nothing.

Doctor Doom is an Eastern European king, beloved by his own people but seen as monstrous by outsiders. He's a monarch in Vlad the Impaler's image, so making Doom a literal descendant of Vlad fits. But "Ultimate" Doom does not live up to his family legacy.

His first "kingdom" is a camp of squatters in Copenhagen. Clad in green rags (instead of a regal cloak), Doom compares his new skin to "Wallachian armor," but he didn't forge it himself. His metal body can form sharp spikes, like the kind on which Vlad impaled his enemies. Reed Richards, much pettier and hotheaded than his classic self, scratches Victor with one of those spikes and leaves a scar: "Impaled on your own sword, Victor."

Vlad is associated with dragons; his father, Vlad II, was a member of the Order of the Dragon knighthood, and known as Vlad Dracul, or "Vlad the Dragon." (Vlad III's infamous moniker can mean "son of the dragon.") "Ultimate" Doom brands his followers with dragon tattoos made up of mind-controlling microfibers.

Dracula can also mean "son of the Devil." Doom's metallic body has cloven hoof legs, echoing goatlike depictions of Satan. But "Dracula" or not, Doom doesn't drink blood — and neither did the real Vlad the Impaler. Pop culture has reforged Vlad into the historical basis for Bram Stoker's "Dracula" and its vampiric villain. Later "Dracula" adaptations like Francis Ford Coppola's 1992 "Dracula" film or the action-horror manga "Hellsing" explicitly depict Dracula as the same person as the historical Vlad.

"Ultimate Fantastic Four" indulges this, too. Victor's father tells his son that Vlad let his enemies believe he drank blood. As Doom, Victor brands his subjects on their necks, and controls them like vampires do familiars.

Dotor Doom echoes both the historical Vlad and the literary Dracula; his fictional nation, Latveria, is a pastiche of the gothic Transylvania seen in "Dracula" movies. (Many "Fantastic Four" adaptations also insert Doom having an infatuation with Sue Storm that he never has had in the comics, like how many "Dracula" films make the Count and Mina Harker lovers, not monster and victim.)

"Dracula" is often paired with Mary Shelley's "Frankenstein," especially thanks to Universal and Hammer's films of both. Doom melds "Dracula" with "Frankenstein." He shares Dr. Frankenstein's name, Victor, and both men's lives are defined by the death of their respective mothers. Victor Frankenstein tried to create life in grief over his mother's death, while the machine that blew up in Doom's face was an attempt to contact the "nether world" and his mother's soul.

As the classic Doom stands in the shadow of these villains, historical and literary, Victor Van Damme in "Ultimate Fantastic Four" stands in Doom's. "Ultimate Doom" almost feels like a purposefully-inverted villain, taking Doom down a peg yet only highlighting the strengths of the original.

Classic Doom was born into a poor Roma tribe. The world told him he was lesser and he made himself into a king to prove it wrong. "Ultimate" Doom was taught from childhood that the world was his birthright, but he isn't strong enough to carry it — like Kendall Roy if he was a Eurotrash super-villain. Classic Doom created an armored second skin to hide himself and his supposedly scarred face from the world. "Ultimate" Doom's flesh hardened like a chrysalis, turned brittle, and cracked off, leaving only metal — as if a heartless tin man with the features of the Devil is all he ever was.

→ Baca artikel asli

The 10 Best War Books of All Time, Ranked

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

There’s a story below that’s older than books as they're currently understood, which speaks to how storytelling has always been a thing culturally, sure, but it also speaks to how war has always been a thing. Throughout the history of literature, stories about war have been significant, important, and dramatically compelling, and then there’s at least one great story about warfare technically from even earlier.

As best as possible, there are books about a range of wars below, and to keep things more interesting, there are a couple of fantasy novels that also count as war books included below, too. What you won’t see here are great non-fiction books about war, nor memoirs or strictly biographical novels. This ranking of the best war books of all time will include some works of historical fiction, though, and there are only a couple of inclusions that are about conflicts of an entirely fictional nature.

Like another book from the 1960s concerned with World War II that'll be mentioned shortly, Catch-22 is famously about how weird, despair-filled, and almost comically absurd war can be, but without the sci-fi elements of that other novel and also with a bit more of a focus on humor. It’s a very dark comedy, though, and the humor is of a surreal nature, too, all of it getting quite exhausting at a point.

Then again, that exhaustion does feel like the point of Catch-22, and at least it’s got a great premise, since it explores the notion that anyone who wants to exit the war must be sensible, and so they can’t request a check for insanity as a way to get out, since doing that demonstrates their sanity. There’s a lot more chaos on top of all that, and the novel is fairly episodic, at a point, albeit in a way that mostly works (and, again, feels deliberate).

All Quiet on the Western Front was written and published, as it just so happens, between the end of World War I and the beginning of World War II. So back then, it wasn’t called World War I, and you'd have to assume people were pretty hopeful there wouldn’t be any more world wars. Plenty of books post-World War II ended up being about that conflict, for fairly obvious reasons.

So, All Quiet on the Western Front, despite being nearly a century old, might well be the ultimate anti-war book about the First World War. The narrative here is what you get in many stories that take an anti-war approach to things, with young people being swept up in a conflict they don’t fully understand until it’s tragically too late, but when it’s done well, it has to be appreciated. And, to All Quiet on the Western Front’s credit, it did this kind of anti-war narrative before a good many other novels and/or movies with similar approaches and stories.

The Aubrey-Maturin series overall concerned the Napoleonic Wars, but the first of 20 (completed) books, Master and Commander, takes place shortly before the outbreak of the conflict proper. Still, like the rest of the series, Master and Commander does concern naval warfare, and then it’s set during the War of the Second Coalition, which was part of the French Revolutionary Wars, and those wars were the prelude to the Napoleonic Wars.

Sorry if that’s simplifying things. It’s not in the spirit of Aubrey-Maturin to simplify things, since Patrick O'Brian’s attention to detail is legendary, and you get a sense of authenticity almost right from the start of the first book, Master and Commander. It’s clearly the best place to start, and you're obviously in luck if you like what you read, seeing as the rest of the series is acclaimed and very readable as well.

The elephant in the room that’s always there when you're talking about A Song of Ice and Fire is that it’s a series that has infamously not finished, and it feels increasingly unlikely that it will. To paraphrase that now-dead meme, we really did get GTA 6 before A Song of Ice and Fire #6. Still, what does exist of the series is impressive, and war is a pretty big part of all the books.

Of those, A Game of Thrones (book #1) is where things happen that really set off the main war of the series, and so book #2, A Clash of Kings, feels the worthiest of being included here. Those potential kings really do clash, with there being a whole War of Five Kings that gets progressively messier and truly feels like the focus of the bulk of this book. It’s not where that conflict ends, by any means, but it feels most accurate to call A Clash of Kings a “war book,” out of all the entries in the series to date.

This is the one that pairs fairly well with Catch-22, since Slaughterhouse-Five is also bold, about World War II, and sometimes darkly funny. It’s also got some unusual sci-fi elements, in case the idea of a sometimes-funny, sometimes-sad, sometimes semi-autobiographical, and sometimes mind-bending book about World War II didn’t sound interesting enough on its own.

Here, a good deal of the book concerns the bombing of Dresden, which is something Kurt Vonnegut himself experienced, so he was a good authority to write on the subject, of course. The time travel and other parts are where it strays away from feeling autobiographical, yet the reliving of war and trauma is authentic, and made more literal with it being genuinely revisited and more than “just” flashed back to. Oh, and Slaughterhouse-Five does all this within a very short duration, all up. It’s wild stuff, and very little else out there is quite like it.

The Winds of War is a massive novel, and yet it’s hard to talk about it without also acknowledging that it’s just one half of an overall story that continued with War and Remembrance. If you want, consider them both worthy of taking up this spot, since they function as one grand family saga that spans World War II quite comprehensively, including the lead-up to it, at least from the U.S. point of view; like, the lead-up to the U.S. joining the conflict.

That’s what book #1 builds to, at least, with War and Remembrance perhaps being more about the war and being in the thick of it. Anyway, both stand out as great pieces of historical fiction, since the broad events here really happened, but many (albeit not all) of the characters are fictional. You do need to devote a lot of time to reading something like The Winds of War, and then even more time to (the even longer) second book, but it’s an ultimately worthwhile endeavor.

There was a lot of talk about the 2026 film adaptation of Homer's The Odyssey changing a good deal of the original text, but the changes there have nothing on all the changes made to Homer's The Iliad with 2004’s Troy. The name was changed there, so maybe that dulled the impact a bit. Also, The Iliad feels like a more difficult story to get right, if you're adapting something, since it’s got more moving parts and a higher number of truly important characters featured throughout.

It’s technically about part of the Trojan War, but not with all the events you might expect, and with fantastical elements (though not as many as you get with The Odyssey). Clearly, The Iliad is important as far as stories about warfare go, and that importance and the overall history/legacy here make it an essential read, even with it being quite complicated and overwhelming (to greater or lesser extents depending on which translation you tackle).

Oh, Gravity’s Rainbow. This is a fairly well-known book for something that’s also remarkably impenetrable and overwhelmingly dense, not to mention incredibly confronting and even disgusting in parts. Those factors could contribute to why it’s well-known, so maybe calling it notorious is more accurate, but maybe all that works in its favor as well. It’s a difficult one to summarize, beyond saying that it’s largely set during the final stages of World War II, and also, there’s a lot of talk about V-2 rockets.

Some people are searching for one particular V-2 rocket, seemingly, and then others aren’t really as tied to what seems to be going on, sort of just dropping in and out of what ends up being a very sprawling and complex novel. Parts of Gravity’s Rainbow are thrilling in an unusual sort of way, yet much of it’s also overwhelming, which is something that can be said about a great many Thomas Pynchon novels. But for capturing the madness of war, and for also being unforgettable (for better or worse), Gravity’s Rainbow does feel like a deservedly controversial classic of sorts.

There isn't much else that can be said about The Lord of the Rings, since if there is a reason for it to be ranked high on any kind of ranking, you do more or less have to put it there. And high. But here, it might be worth admitting that it’s more of a fantasy epic, and as far as war books go, it’s not conventional, seeing as the conflict here is a fantastical one, and the war’s entirely fictional and all (not like in Slaughterhouse-Five, where the war was real, but a sci-fi/fictional spin was put on things).

And then it also differs from A Clash of Kings, because the warfare in that book felt a bit more authentic, with less by way of magic and non-human creatures and all. But with The Lord of the Rings, it does feel worth emphasizing The Return of the King as the third volume of the overall epic, and the one where the war stuff really takes center-stage, seeing as it does end up having particularly big battles, and the whole War of the Ring comes to its inevitable end.

War and Peace is famously long, so maybe that’s all you’ve heard about it, but there are so many reasons to read it, even if you're usually put off by longer novels. Yet the length can’t really be ignored, with Leo Tolstoy going in-depth on parts of the Napoleonic Wars, all the while also having stretches of the book be more concerned with melodrama and romance, and then Tolstoy can’t help but go off on pretty much non-fiction-ish (or essay-type) rants about history, too.

So, it’s famously more than just a long book, and it’s varied enough that even calling it a long novel in the traditional sense might be misleading, since it has aspirations beyond just telling a story. Still, that story is compelling, and then the more unexpected parts are also super interesting in different ways. War and Peace is genuinely absorbing for all those many, many pages, and while it’s hard to commit to something like this initially, dedicating oneself to the whole epic starts to feel rewarding much earlier than one might expect.

You Know Animal-Print Rugs, But Do You Know Animal Pictorials? (2026)

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The oldest known pile rug—pulled from a frozen tomb in Siberia in 1949—features a procession of 24 fallow deer and a parade of men on horseback. Some 2,500 years after its creation, animal illustrations are making their way back onto the floor. A serpent coils through the Snake and Pomegranate rug by Christopher Farr and Studio Shamshiri in Lucy Hale’s Los Angeles home, and a Tibetan tiger stretches across Demi Lovato’s modern farmhouse and Ethan Cook’s SoHo loft. These aren’t spots or stripes standing in for a neutral—they’re meant to be noticed and discussed. “Animal print is a texture; an animal motif is a story,” says AD100 designer Pavitra Rajaram, who recently designed an animal-themed collection for Jaipur Living called Majnun. “I think people are tired of decoration that says nothing and are looking for objects that carry meaning.”

Rajaram is describing a literal animal rug, also known as an animal pictorial. These playful floor coverings often feature a bucolic scene of several animals or a single animal form woven into the textile, and they come in styles ranging from avant-garde and surrealist to Art Deco and folk-inspired.

“What we’re seeing now is a shift toward rugs where the whole animal is part of the design and has a role in the composition,” says Kerri Anne Besse, design director at rug brand Tumble. “The oversized, blown-up animal print feels dated.”

As for styling, Sophie Salata, head of brand at Vinterior, recommends that designers let the pictorials stand on their own. “I’d be inclined to treat the rug almost as a piece of art, allowing it to become a focal point rather than trying to make everything around it match,” she says. Pamela Marshall, creative director at artisanal rug brand Patterson Flynn, agrees with this approach: These designs are more than backdrops.

Reformation's nine-piece rug collection with Les Éditions de Tapis includes depictions of cats, swans, and tigers.

Recently, brands have been launching their own interpretations of animal pictorials. Last week, Reformation launched its first-ever home collection, a collaboration with Les Éditions de Tapis that features hand-drawn swans and cats. At Milan Design Week this year, Fornasetti launched a surrealist-inspired line of rugs with CC-Tapis depicting slithering serpents from the luxury designer’s archive. A few months earlier, Ruggable released its collaboration with Scalamandré to bring the upscale design house’s iconic zebras to more homes. And in October 2025, Nordic Knots launched its Swedish Grace collection, including the Grace 03 Fauna rug, which shows a wildlife scene. Looking further back, Beni’s serpent rugs have been a designer favorite since 2020.

One of the earlier examples of literal animal rugs is a collection between longstanding collaborators Patterson Flynn and AD100 designers Charlap Hyman & Herrero. It grew from a one-off rug into a full collection of graphic natural-and-black silhouettes that launched in 2018 and continues to expand. “Areas of inspiration for these come from Calder’s simplified animal forms, the surrealists’ play on scale, and 17th-century cabinets of curiosity, to name a few,” says Marshall, who credits Hyman’s “encyclopedic” design references for the originality. We would be remiss not to mention Diane von Furstenberg’s long-running collaboration with the Rug Company, which has been producing the iconic Climbing Leopard rug since 2005.

Rugs from Charlap Hyman & Herrero x Patterson Flynn’s collaboration.

The Rug Company x Diane von Furstenberg Climbing Leopard rug in emerald.

These rugs’ rich history might be part of their renewed appeal. Animals have been a subject for as long as people have made pictures. The oldest known figurative cave painting—found in Indonesia and dated to at least 51,200 years ago—shows a wild pig with three human figures.

“Animals have shaped our textiles and rugs for longer than we think,” says Bella Valenzia, founder of Pelican House, pointing out that animal forms have appeared in tapestries and wall coverings, and as functional home objects, such as pelt and hide rugs used for warmth and comfort, since the earliest days of humankind. “We like to think it is part of a wider return to folk-inspired, storytelling textiles, with a sense of nostalgia,” she adds. Pelican House’s rug Frith references the old English countryside, where horses have long been at the heart of rural life.

Various animals, real and mythical, carry their own significance across cultures, and their meaning can deeply influence a home’s design. For example, a pictorial of a lion, which signifies power in certain cultures, adds a very different element to a space than a fish, which can signify good luck, notes Jahanshah “Josh” Nazmiyal of Rug & Kilim. Veronica Renton, senior vintage and antiques specialist at 1stDibs, adds that peacocks often signal nobility and immortality.

Plenty of designers still reach for the originals: While the contemporary versions of these rugs are having a moment, their earlier counterparts remain relevant. “I particularly love antique Tibetan tiger rugs and Persian and Chinese rugs featuring guardian lions, dragons, and all manner of mythical beasts,” says Tiffany Duggan, founder of Studio Duggan.

The Tiger Rug by Studio Ashby in Sophie Ashby’s former home in London.

Where these rugs live in the home is likewise important. Naturally, the pieces work well in spaces that are meant to be playful, like nurseries and children’s rooms. For example, in this garden apartment designed by Rebecca Benichou and Florence Jallet of Batiik Studio, the Toulemonde Bochart Lion rug brings a spot of levity to a windowless craft room. In the bedroom of Kate Upton and Justin Verlander’s daughter, Genevieve, designed by John Ruggiero, the Doing Goods Blooming Black Bear Rug provides a spot of whimsy. For double the fun, designer Olivia Song put two animal rugs, the Doing Goods Pinky Lion and Pinky Leopard, in her nephew’s room in this Sag Harbor beach home.

That said, these rugs work in more adult spaces, too. “In the [primary] bedroom of one of our London projects, we used the beautiful Snake and Pomegranate rug,” says Duggan. “I love the way the snake weaves its way around the bed, creating a focal point and bringing a little humor and unexpected character to an otherwise calm space.”

Designer Rayman Boozer from Apartment 48 has a rug with a large tiger on it from the Rug Company in his own home. “Animal motifs can sometimes feel juvenile, but there’s something refreshing about bringing that sense of playfulness into a sophisticated space,” he says.

Rayman Boozer’s home, featuring an Apartment 48 tiger rug.

Steven Loloi, co-principal at Loloi, agrees. Speaking of Loloi’s Roam collection that launched last month and features cheetahs, zebras, elephants, and giraffes in neutral colors, he highlights the surprising versatility of the pieces, which he says can fit just as nicely in a bedroom or living room as in a lounge or den. “I think we’re seeing people become more confident in their design choices and a little less concerned with playing it safe,” he says. “Why not have a leopard or zebra in your living room?”

It’s worth noting that these rugs aren’t for everyone, and some designers avoid any type of animal rug because of the hunting culture they reference. Others, though, embrace them as a way to reimagine humanity’s relationship to animals and commit to living better by them.

Rajaram’s Majnun collection for Jaipur Living does just that with its contemporary expression of “shikargahs,” the enclosed forests where kings hunted at leisure, which are celebrated in Iranian and Mughal design. “Instead of being chased, the animals are in harmony with each other, frolicking freely in the forest. It’s a celebration of coexistence rather than conquest,” she says. The Charlap Hyman & Herrero x Patterson Flynn collection had a similar ambition: “Our Tigre and Oso nod to traditional hide floor coverings; they give you the drama of the trophy rug without the trophy,” notes Marshall.

On the surface, the return of these animal-forward rugs might have looked like a change in aesthetics, but it’s really much more anthropological. “We have always gravitated toward pieces with animals, and even in modern production, history is repeating itself,” says Nazmiyal. Some 2,500 years after their first spotting, these animal-clad rugs are back home.

Timothée Chalamet Declares the Official Start of Puffer Season

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Like any true New Yorker, Timothée Chalamet has built himself a deep bench of cold-weather staples. We’re talking puffers on puffers on puffers—hailing from The North Face, Supreme, Thug Club and beyond—paired with all manner of boots, including Visvim lace-ups, Red Wing moc toes, and multiple pairs of Timbs (of both the OG Wheat and custom Chrome Hearts varieties.) For all his fashion-guy flourishes, Chalamet’s winter outfits generally have the chops to handle a brick New York sidewalk come February. So yesterday, even though it was still a balmy 68 degrees in NYC, it was no surprise to see Chalamet cheering on his beloved Knicks at Madison Square Garden in yet another excellent down-filled jacket and blizzard-ready boots.

Sitting courtside, the Dune star wore a combination familiar to just about anyone who’s endured winter on the East Coast. The jacket was a dusty pink number from Goldwin, the cult Japanese outdoors brand founded back in 1950. It cuts a sharp silhouette despite packing some serious down insulation, and the windproof, water-resistant nylon is designed to ward off rain, sleet, and snow. The muted exterior has a subtle sheen—not quite Cam’ron pink, but certainly not your average black puffer. Over the past couple years, pink has become one of Chalamet’s signature colors: Last January, he paired a bubblegum Telfar puffer with an Arc’teryx toque and a Chanel purse like it was no big deal; less than a month later, he popped up at the Berlin Film Festival in head-to-toe pastel Chrome Hearts. This time out, he left the handbag at home but topped things off with a Knicks championship hat.

And then there were the boots. At first glance, they could pass for a pair of Uggs, thanks to the familiar shape and cozy suede uppers. (It wouldn’t have been the first time he wore a pair of the Aussie surfer classics courtside.) But these pull-ons were, in fact, from menswear favorite Merrell, built for some truly cold and ugly weather. The outer suede is water-resistant, the lining is insulated, and the rubber outsoles boast some serious traction. Plus, the very gorp-y utility cord at the heel lets you cinch them extra tight. Like the advanced-level dresser he is, Chalamet tucked his baggy black pants into the boots, making sure his footwear got a chance to really shine.

Once again, Chalamet has turned practical winter clothes into an A-list look. While a pink puffer might not be every guy’s idea of cold-weather dressing, there’s something to be said for a winter coat with a little personality. Was celebrity row’s resident heartthrob a few weeks early with this ensemble? Sure, but when that first serious snowfall hits, he’ll be extra ready.

NYC Mayor’s Office Hires First Executive Director of Music

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Rachael Pazdan will help the city support musicians by serving as a liaison between the government and the music industry.

The NYC mayor’s office of media and entertainment (MOME) hired Rachael Pazdan as its first executive director of music. She will report to Rafael Espinal, commissioner of the NYC MOME.

“New York City has shaped the sound of music for generations,” Espinal said in a statement. “But being a great city for music should also mean being a great city for the people who make it. That’s why building our music portfolio is a priority for me. Rachael Pazdan brings the experience and relationships to help us get it right. Together, we’ll listen to the industry and work toward making live performances easier, expanding opportunities across all five boroughs, and connecting young New Yorkers to careers in music. Our goal is simple: make New York City the best place in the world to create music, perform music, and make a living in music.”

Pazdan will build on the city’s New York Music Month and its newly launched Music Production Bootcamp, as well as Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s commitment to increase access to entertainment experiences for NYC residents.

Since launching in 2017, New York Music Month has grown to host 38 talks, 21 performances, a conference and a free rehearsal series that offers more than 2,000 hours of free rehearsal space across the city in four locations.

Parzdan has worked with indie music promoter AdHoc, helping the company become a full-service creative agency that served clients including Bryant Park, Pitchfork, A2IM and BandLab. While at the company, she also helped promote events including Global Citizen in New York City and Johannesburg.

She also served as a booker for numerous independent venues across New York City, including Public Records, The Bell House and Le Poisson Rouge, and produced the short-form docuseries Stories from the Stage, which shared stories from New York City’s independent venues and promoters during the pandemic.
